The clause order is now the claim it should always have been — the deadline answers *has this window started*, so it is asked only after `usedPct > 0` has failed to settle the question. `windowRemaining` already handled a missing reset (it uses it only for the roll-over test) and `deadline()` simply skips a running window that states none.

**② A STORED VERDICT OUTLIVED THE READING IT DESCRIBED.** `state` is a verdict the projection computes from a (usedPct, resetsAt, measuredAt) triple — but it is emitted onto the legacy bucket, and that object is SPREAD forward by every producer (`{...(cache[ev.kind] || {})}` in rate-limit-capture, `bump()` in markLimitBanner). So a producer that re-measured a bucket carried the old verdict onto the new numbers. Measured: a 5h bucket stamped `unknown` while it had no reset kept that stamp through a fully dated 95 % reading (`{remaining: 80}` instead of 5 — this is what made ① permanent rather than transient); the routine post-weekly-roll shape, correctly stamped `empty`, kept it while three real `rate_limit_event`s climbed the 7d to 90 % and the Fable cap to 100 % — `{remaining: 90}` instead of 0, `weeklyDeadline` **null**, `bucketRems` listing only the 5h row. That is **inc-msof8i22 / 2.305.0 re-opened**: a spent model cap invisible to the pool's exhaustion test, and invisible to the estimator too, since `usage-anchors` filters on the same predicate. The fix is TWO HALVES and they are not redundant — each catches a case the other structurally cannot. The PRODUCERS drop the verdict whenever they re-state a number (`restated()` in rate-limit-capture; unconditional in `bump`, which always restates): this is the only half that catches a re-measurement DOWN to 0 % onto a reset that has since PINNED — the window really did open, the member barely used it, `usedPct > 0` says nothing, and keeping the `empty` stamp costs that member its real weekly deadline. The READER keeps a clock-free belt in `fromLegacy` — `if (STATES.includes(b.state) && !(usedPct > 0))` — because a stated spend refutes `empty` by definition and needs no clock; that half heals a stamp arriving from a FILE an older build wrote (on the next write) or from a producer nobody enumerated. **What must NOT change**: a `status`-only event re-states no number, the reading is unchanged, and the verdict SURVIVES — that is the entire reason the stamp is trusted rather than re-derived, since a legacy bucket read back off disk usually carries only the FILE's `fetchedAt`, a later clock that would compute `elapsed > jitter` and flip a genuinely empty window to `'running'` — B-8b12 backwards.

**③ THE DERIVED VIEW WAS MORE OPTIMISTIC THAN THE SET IT PROJECTS.** `scopedWeekly` gives a model limit's BUDGET window a home and the plan limit's own windows have theirs — but a model limit's FIVE-HOUR window had none at all: `isBudgetKind` excludes `'5h'` from the scoped projection, and `fiveHour` was `toBucket(windowOfKind(plan, '5h'))`, the plan limit alone. `account-pool-auto` reads the VIEW, not the accessor. Driven with the two real codex push shapes censused from this instance's buffers (plan 7d 33 %; `codex_bengalfox` / "GPT-5.3-Codex-Spark" 5h 90 % + 7d 10 %): the accessor answered `{remaining: 10, by: 'codex_bengalfox', kind: '5h'}` and the pool answered **`{remaining: 67}`** — a 57-point drift at the exact seam B-9213 is about, and a **REGRESSION**: the base commit's last-writer-wins collapse happened to keep the Spark 5h and answered 10. §⑫ had only ever tested the 0 % direction ("a Spark bucket at 0 % never grants headroom the plan bucket lacks"); the inverse was untested and broken. `fiveHour` now means what every reader has always used it for — THE BINDING BURST CONSTRAINT on the account, the min over every counting 5-hour window in the set. One rule, no backend branch (claude's model caps are weekly-only, so it can only ever pick the plan window there), windows that make no claim are not candidates, and with no counting 5h window anywhere the plan's own bucket is still emitted so the panel keeps seeing a limit the vendor reported.

**ROUND 3, SECOND PASS — THE FIX FOR ③ CONTAINED ③ (found by driving ③'s own property with a passed reset).** "The binding burst constraint" was first spelled as *the highest `usedPct`*, which is not what binding means. A window whose reset has PASSED rolled over since we read it, so its stale utilization means nothing and it is FULL — that is `windowRemaining`'s rule, and `bucketRemaining` (the pool's) applies it to whatever bucket the projection emits. So a 90 %-spent-but-rolled-over model window DISPLACED a plan window with a real 50 % left, and the view came out **more optimistic than the set** (measured: accessor 50 %, pool 90 %) — the exact defect ③ exists to prevent, living inside the fix for it, and invisible because ③'s matrix used only future resets. The ranking is now `windowRemaining` on the SAME clock `remaining()`/`deadline()` take (`toLegacyView(set, {nowSec})`, defaulting to now), so the invariant holds by construction rather than by a second, weaker ordering. And ties now go to the PLAN window because it is SEEDED first rather than left to iteration order — every window of a set read long after it was measured ties at 100, so without the seed a Spark bucket displaced the plan limit again, undoing §⑭'s own invariant. **The lesson is about the guard, not the ordering**: a property is worth exactly the shapes it is driven over, so the matrix now carries a rolled-over window and §⑯d asserts that the property goes red on this mutant — it could not before. Mutation-test your own new legs, and when a leg compares two answers, check they were asked with the same clock (the first version compared a real-clock projection against a fixed-clock accessor, which would have measured the leg's inconsistency rather than the product's).

**ROUND-3 INVARIANTS.** **A predicate's clause order is a claim about which fact is decisive** — put the fact that alone settles the question first, and let the others narrow what is left. **A stored verdict is only as good as the reading it describes**; the producer that replaced the numbers is the one that must drop it, and the reader's belt is for the files and producers nobody enumerated — **two mechanisms need two negative controls, each pointed at a case only it can catch** (a control both halves satisfy measures nothing; round 3's first draft had exactly that, and rewriting the controls is what found the DOWN-to-0 % case). **A lossy projection must lose CONSERVATIVELY, and that is a property to drive, not a list of fields to remember**: the guard against the next missing window kind is `accountRemaining(toLegacyView(set)) <= remaining(set)` over a matrix of shapes, with the plan-only projection as its control. And a census must not flatter itself — a reader that calls the model's predicates but consumes the derived view is `view-only`, not `migrated`; it is exactly as right as the projection, which is what makes that property test load-bearing.

**GATES.** test-quota-model 179 (§⑮ drives the REAL shipped tool over a file carrying `limits`, with the round-1 literal as the negative control; §⑯ the three round-3 defects + §⑯d the one the fix for ③ introduced — each with a PATCHED COPY of the real module as its negative control, the patch asserted to hit, and the view-vs-accessor property over a 10-shape matrix that now carries a rolled-over burst window), test-readings-attribution 445 (§15 the rejection is judged at the write + two controls for the cases that must NOT change, §18d the two-bucket shape with a PRE-FIX patched copy of the engine, §18c the strength table with round 1 as its control), test-auto-resume-loop 205, test-rate-limit-capture 46, test-auto-resume 222, test-pool-auto 74, test-account-pool 33, test-account-verdicts 45, test-usage-anchors 12, test-usage-estimator 83, test-quota-source 112, test-codex-pool 61, test-new-member-wake 119, test-login-expiry 257, test-migrations 14, test-vendor-whitelist 63 (zero new vendor surface), test-architecture 110. Mutation-tested: the strength split (6 red), the per-record call (the §18d control), the statusline spread (the §⑮ control), the re-based wiring pin (2 red on a fresh slot resolution), the banner redirect, the banner's signal key (which reproduces the dropped-own-wall regression), and the ordering of the proof's clear.

**ROUND 4 — A FREE MEMBER IS NOT AN UNKNOWN ONE (2026-09-09, the round-3 verifier; reproduced end to end against the base commit before being fixed).** Round 3 fenced the not-yet-started window off from every answer a window gives. But a window answers TWO questions and only one of them is B-8b12's. **"When does this budget end?"** — an empty window has no answer; its reset is `now + duration` on every read, so ranking on it makes it the earliest deadline forever and arms auto-resume on a number that has already moved. `bucketCounts` / `countingWindows` exist for that question and are UNCHANGED. **"How much is left?"** — an empty window has a precise answer: ALL of it, because the vendor STATED a spend of 0 %. Dropping that turns a definite claim into ignorance, and ignorance is not neutral here: it is `known:false`, which the pool ranks at `UNKNOWN_REMAINING_PCT` (50) and `quotaVerdict` reports as "no usage data" (`usable:null`).

**MEASURED.** A brand-new claude account whose panel prints `0% used` on both plan buckets — the ORDINARY shape, since the `· resets` clause of a panel line is optional, `refreshViaCliPanel` deliberately refuses to project the 5-hour reset, and a fresh account has no prior reset to carry forward — read back to the pool as "no usage data": `accountRemaining` `{remaining:100,known:true}` → `{null,false}`, `quotaVerdict` `usable:true` → `usable:null` (so the engine's own pool gate, `verdicts.find((x) => x.v.usable === true)`, finds nobody and reports the WHOLE POOL blocked), `rankPoolMembers` eff 100 → 50, and `decidePoolSwitch` off an EXHAUSTED member (4 % left) went from `{to:'new', toRemaining:100, reason:'exhausted'}` to `{to:null, reason:'no-settleable'}` — the pool keeps spending on a spent member while a fully free one sits beside it. A REGRESSION against the base commit, and the branch's own backfill proves it on real data: over a copy of this instance's `data/usage-cache` (12 scanned / 12 stamped / 31 limits / idempotent) it flips exactly ONE of twelve files — `__global_codex__.json`, the only one whose every window is fresh, i.e. the measured B-8b12 shape — from `{usable:true,known:true}` to `{usable:null,known:false}`. The other eleven are untouched because their 7d is running, which is why every gate suite stayed green.

**THE FIX = TWO QUESTIONS, TWO PREDICATES**, written side by side in the PURE module so one rule in two spellings cannot drift: `windowStatesSpend` / `bucketStatesSpend` (`num(usedPct)` / `num(utilization)` — `num()` and not `Number()`, because `Number(null)` is 0 and "the vendor said nothing" must never read as "the vendor said zero"), `remaining()` mins over `spendingWindows`, and `bucketRemaining` gates on the legacy twin. Measured on this instance — 35 cache buckets, 14,286 anchor buckets, ZERO missing or null — so the shape where the new predicate is stricter than the pre-model one does not occur, and refusing it is the direction that cannot invent headroom. Including empty windows cannot go the other way: `remaining` is a MIN, so a 100 %-free bucket can never lift another limit's 5 %; it can only flip `known:false` → `known:true`. B-9213's harm was a Spark push OVERWRITING the plan limit and the per-limit MERGE is what fixes that. **§⑫'s own negative control had said so since round 1** — *"an empty bucket is 100 % free, so it never HID an exhaustion — the harm was always the deadline"* — the branch's own test file stated the principle while the code applied the exclusion to both answers.

**TWO SECOND-ORDER DEFECTS, both found by this round's OWN controls.** ① `toLegacyView`'s `toBucket` fabricated `utilization: 0` for a window with no stated spend, so a producer that stated only a reset came back off disk as `{utilization: 0}` and the pool read 100 % free and KNOWN while the typed accessor beside it correctly answered "no claim" — the view MORE optimistic than the set, which is exactly what §⑯c exists to prevent and could NOT see, because its property compared two KNOWN answers. The bucket is still emitted (a limit the vendor reported must stay visible) but carries no `utilization`, and the property grew the clause `(v.known && !a.known)`: claiming to know is itself an optimism. ② A `bucketRems` row publishes its `resetsAt` only when the bucket may name a deadline — the row exists because the bucket states a REMAINING. Today a 100 %-free bucket can never reach the `dead` list `quotaVerdict` turns into `blockedUntil`, so this is safe by CONSTRUCTION rather than by that argument, which is the point: the argument would otherwise have to be re-made by every future reader of the field.

**INVARIANTS.** **One predicate per question.** A window is evidence about two different things and a single fence answers only one of them correctly; before reusing a predicate, name the question it was written for. **Ignorance is not the safe default when a fact was stated** — "no usage data" costs a member the ranking, the usability verdict and its settleability, so refusing to read a stated 0 % is a money decision, not a conservative one. **When one harm ends up with two independent belts, each owes its own negative control**: round 3's `state` machinery and this split now both hold the pool's remaining honest, so §⑯a/§⑯b's controls were re-pointed at what only THEY protect (the ANCHOR stream — the estimator's whole input and the corpus the window fingerprint identifies accounts from — and the weekly deadline), and new §⑯a-x/§⑯b-x remove BOTH to reproduce round 3's own published numbers (80 % free; the spent Fable cap invisible at 90 % with a null deadline). **A property that bounds A against B guards the SEAM, never the shared rule underneath it** — §⑯c could not see this defect because the view and the accessor AGREED; the rule itself needs a leg that names an answer. **And the test file's own words are evidence**: a negative control whose message contradicts the code beside it is a bug report nobody read. Mutation-tested against the product source: reverting `bucketRemaining` → 14 red, `remaining()` → 5 red, `toBucket` → 2 red, the `bucketRems` reset gate → 1 red, restored → 0. **scripts/test-quota-model.mjs 214** (+ test-pool-auto 74, test-account-verdicts 45, test-readings-attribution 445, test-auto-resume-loop 205, test-spend-paths 261 all green).

**ROUND 5 — WHO MEASURED THIS NUMBER, AND WHO MAY SAY A LIMIT IS GONE (2026-09-09, the round-4 verifier; both reproduced against a copy of this instance's own post-migration cache file before anything was changed).** Both defects live in `writeCacheObject`'s LIFT, and they are the two halves of one gap: the lift is handed a LEGACY object, and a legacy object is ONE reading spread over a carry-forward of everything else. It could not tell the halves apart, so it stamped the whole file with this producer's name and clock (①), and `mergeLimitSets` — correctly, for a producer that knows about one bucket — let nobody ever say a limit had stopped existing (②).

**① PER-LIMIT PROVENANCE WAS OVERWRITTEN ON EVERY CLAUDE WRITE — the write path's own guarantee #4, and the panel rows added to enforce it, both lying.** `captureRateLimitEvent` spreads the identity's freshest file, changes the ONE bucket its event names, and hands the whole object here; `liftCacheObject` then rebuilds EVERY limit on it via `fromLegacy`, which stamps `source: srcName, fetchedAt: at` on all of them, and `mergeLimit` prefers the copy with the newer (or equal — the test is `>=`) `fetchedAt`, so the freshly-clocked copy always wins. Measured with the real modules over a copy of this instance's `usage-cache/sub-*.json` (post-migration, three limits, anonymised). BEFORE: `plan: on-demand@1788974610137 | model:fable: on-demand@1788974610137 | overage: on-demand@1788911228197`. ONE `rate_limit_event` about the five-hour bucket — a record that says nothing whatever about Fable — AFTER: **all three re-stamped `rate-limit-event`, the Fable cap dated to now, and its window's `measuredAt` moved onto a `utilization: 1` nobody re-measured.** Every claude producer reaches it (`src/rate-limit-capture.js`, the limit-banner mark — so the panel said the Fable cap came from a limit hit — `writeUsageCacheForKey`, `refreshViaCliPanel`, the host harvest); the codex path is CORRECT because it passes a TYPED SET. User-visible through `limitRows` → `via {source}` / `Updated {ago}` in `usage-meter`, i.e. the standing rule *"a producer WE SHIP always stamps its own `source` at the write"* and *"'Updated 3min ago' alone is the sentence the readings-by-slot incident could not survive"* — violated by the feature added to enforce them. 659 green asserts said nothing, because the §17 panel leg in test-readings-attribution feeds a HAND-BUILT `limits` array and never goes through the write path.

**THE FIX IS A DERIVATION, NOT A LIST.** "Which fields did I measure?" asked of each producer is a preserve list, and a preserve list is the single tool that has now failed on this store SIX times (`scopedWeekly`, the org identity, `spend`, `corroborated`, the established window, `limits`). The FILE can answer it without being asked: PURE `windowClaimKey` / `limitClaimKey` / `sameLimitClaim` (quota-model) spell what a reading CLAIMS — the numbers, deliberately excluding `measuredAt` and `state`, which are the provenance halves — and `carryUnmeasuredLimits` (the write path, lift-only) hands back the PREVIOUS object for anything whose claim is unchanged. **Granularity is the WINDOW, not the limit** — the claude plan limit holds two windows and a `rate_limit_event` names exactly one bucket, so resolving per limit still re-clocked a week-old 7-day number with this event's clock, and `windowState` reads that clock (found by this round mutation-testing its own first leg; it has its own single-mechanism control). It is **not** applied to a typed `set`: a producer that builds a LimitSet states exactly the limits it carries — that is what the typed shape is FOR — and re-deriving one there would be a second, weaker answer to a question the producer already answered. **The direction it errs in is deliberate**: a producer that genuinely re-measured a number and got the same value keeps the OLDER age (the row says "measured then", not "confirmed now"), which is the conservative half — a number that looks staler than it is costs a re-read; a stale number wearing a fresh timestamp is the incident. The FILE-level `fetchedAt` is untouched, so every freshness-ranked mechanism (the anchor sweep, the identity-group "freshest file" base) sees exactly what it saw before — **the file's `source` and a limit's `source` answer two different questions** ("who wrote this file last" vs "who produced THIS number"), and after this fix they may legitimately disagree.

**② A LIMIT THE VENDOR STOPS REPORTING WAS RESURRECTED FOR EVER, WITH ITS LAST NUMBER.** `mergeLimitSets` keeps every previous limitId unconditionally — the whole of the B-9213 fix, and right for the producers that know about one limit — so NOBODY could say a limit was gone. Reproduced on the real write path + the real pool: two model-scoped caps (`OldModel` at 100 %, `Fable` at 10 %), then the next panel read naming only `Fable` — on disk `scopedWeekly` still carries `OldModel` at 1, `toLegacyView` re-emits it, and `accountRemaining` answers **`{remaining:0, known:true}`** for an account whose live reading says 80. The base commit answers 80: the producer's list landed verbatim there, so this is a **REGRESSION of this branch's own making**. Harm is bounded by the stale cap's own `resetsAt` (`bucketRemaining` returns 100 once it passes) — up to one week of a healthy member excluded — and the trigger is a cap retirement or rename, which is why it is latent here: across all eight anchor streams on this instance the only scoped name that has ever appeared is `Fable`. `replace: true` is not the answer; it is documented as repair-only and it drops EVERYTHING, including limits this producer never had an opinion about.

**SO THE PRODUCER SAYS IT, AND ONLY A PRODUCER THAT CAN.** `authoritativeScopes` names the scopes THIS write enumerated; a prev limit of such a scope that the write does not name is RETIRED, loudly (removing a limit removes a CLAIM, and this one gates the pool). It is passed at exactly the seven sites whose OWN parse produced the list — `refreshViaCliPanel`, the bare-token ⟳, the four host ⟳ legs and the engine's control-channel `get_usage` probe — each gated on `u.scopedWeekly?.length` / `parsed.scopedWeekly?.length`, i.e. on the list THIS read produced and never on the preserve-merged object (all six of those call sites carry `merged.scopedWeekly = u.scopedWeekly?.length ? … : prev.scopedWeekly`, so a carried-forward list states nothing about what the vendor still reports). The single-bucket producers, the shipped statusline and the migration never claim it, and **forgetting to pass it leaves today's behaviour** — a stale cap lingers until its own reset — which is the safe direction to fail in.

**ROUND-5 INVARIANTS.** **A producer may only stamp provenance on a number it actually took — and when the producer cannot tell you which those were, DERIVE it from the file instead of asking for a list.** After the sixth loss on one preserve list, the answer is not a seventh entry: a claim key over the numbers turns "did I measure this?" from a promise into a property. **Provenance has the granularity of the thing that was measured**, and here that is the WINDOW, not the limit — a rule resolved one level too coarse still re-clocks a number nobody looked at. **The file's `source` and a limit's `source` are different questions and are allowed to disagree.** **A merge that can only ever ADD means nobody can ever say a thing is GONE** — give the right to retire to the one producer that enumerates, gate it on that producer's OWN parse, and make forgetting it the harmless direction. **A number that looks staler than it is costs a re-read; a stale number wearing a fresh timestamp is the incident** — when a rule must err, name which way and say why. **And a leg that feeds a hand-built store bypasses the code that fills it**: both defects lived under 659 green asserts because the panel-provenance leg built its `limits` array by hand — drive the REAL producer through the REAL write path, or the store's own rules are untested. Mutation-tested against the product source: removing the carried-forward rule → 7 red, removing the retirement → 4 red, resolving per LIMIT instead of per WINDOW → 2 red, removing one call site's wiring → 1 red (the pin), restored → 0. **scripts/test-quota-model.mjs 249** (§⑰a/b/c, each with a PATCHED COPY of the real module as its negative control and the patch asserted to hit, plus a POSITIVE control that a real `rate_limit_event` retires nothing) + test-readings-attribution 445, test-rate-limit-capture 46, test-pool-auto 74, test-account-verdicts 45, test-usage-anchors 12, test-quota-source 112, test-auto-resume 222, test-auto-resume-loop 205, test-new-member-wake 119, test-spend-paths 261, test-architecture 110 — all green.


## THE INBOX ✓ SLID THE NEXT ROW UNDER THE POINTER (inc-mtw02kbq-kj96, 2.369.90)

Owner: "inbox里点击对勾后改变了这个对话的消息数量会导致位置变化，当我想连续点击时造成误点". The "For you" popup re-rendered from scratch on every `user-todos-updated` broadcast; a ✓ moved its row out of the open groups (and shrank the group), so the rows below slid up and the second rapid click at the same screen position hit a different item (bundle: four ✓ at y≈365 within 1.4 s, a broadcast between each).

FIX = an append-only layout for the popup's lifetime (PURE `src/lib/user-todos-layout.js`): `openLayout` from the sorted groups on open, `nextLayout` keeps every known id in its slot — resolved rows rendered in place, dimmed and struck through with ↺ — appends new ids at the end of their group, drops purged ids; `entriesFor` renders it; the group count shows open rows only and the "Recently resolved" tail skips rows still holding their slot above. The sorted order is rebuilt on the next open (any render while the popup is hidden drops the layout).

不变量=**a list the user is clicking INTO must not reflow under the pointer on the click's own consequence** — resolve in place, re-sort on the next open. Negative control = the pre-fix grouping (`todos.open` → groups), which demonstrably slides row 3 into row 2's slot. Gate: test-user-todos-layout 12 (incident replay, reopen-in-place, arrivals append, close rebuilds, the pre-fix control, wiring pins).

## THE TEST SUITE WROTE A FABRICATED CONVERSATION INTO THE REAL ~/.claude, AND THE PRODUCTION LEDGER BILLED IT (2026-09-09, owner: a Fable conversation, and Fable usage, that never happened)

**WHAT THE ARTIFACTS SAY.** `data/usage-history/events-2026-{07,08,09}.ndjson` on this instance held **79,533 rows** for two session ids nobody ever started — `e2e00000-0000-4000-8000-000000000001` and `…0002` — every one `"model":"claude-fable-5"`, `"acct":null`, `"atype":"global"`, most with `"cwd":null`. Beside them, 245 more rows whose `cwd` is a throwaway `/tmp/vs-…` directory. `_cursors.json` (5.2 MB, 30,424 entries) carried **222** entries naming `~/.claude/projects/-tmp-vs-chatpage-test-*` and `-tmp-vs-chat-e2e-cwd-*` files that have not existed for weeks. The two sids are `SID` in `scripts/test-chat-paging.mjs` and `scripts/test-minimap-jump.mjs`.

**THE MECHANISM, IN THREE FACTS.** ① Those two suites build a synthetic transcript — hand-written `assistant` records that name a model and carry a `usage` block although no API request ever happened — and wrote it to `path.join(os.homedir(), '.claude', 'projects', …)`. They had to: they spawn a worktree server with `env: { ...process.env, PORT, VIBESPACE_SKIP_AGENT_HOOKS }` and **no `HOME`**, and a server can only discover a transcript that lives under the home it is RUNNING with. ② While the suite runs, the machine's PRODUCTION VibeSpace instance is walking that same directory — `src/usage-walker.js` reads every `assistant` record with a `message.usage` and appends a permanent ledger row. ③ `acct` is null on all of them, so `costBetweenMulti` files them under the machine login `__global__` and counts them into the `costSince` of its anchor pairs — i.e. into the **learned burn rate the quota estimator spends against** (`src/usage-anchors.js` → `rates.json` → `src/usage-estimator.js`). The Usage window's Fable row, and the estimator's idea of how fast this machine burns, were both partly fiction.

**THE WINDOW IS SECONDS AND THE DAMAGE IS FOREVER.** Both suites DO clean up: `process.on('exit', cleanup)` removes the project dir on the happy path. That is why the directory is almost never there when you look — and why nobody found it by looking. The fixture exists for the suite's runtime (44 s for test-chat-paging, 12 s for test-minimap-jump), which is all the production instance's 3-minute rescan and its per-request walk need. Caught in the act while this fix was being written: at 14:25 UTC `-tmp-vs-chatpage-test-620924` appeared in `~/.claude/projects` (another worktree running master's copy of the suite), and at 14:26 UTC the production ledger grew by 5,406 rows — one full run's worth. `cleanup` also runs from `'exit'` only, which **does not fire for a default-terminated SIGINT/SIGTERM**; measured with a same-source A/B (SIGTERM at 8 s, throwaway HOME both arms): the pre-fix suite left **2,056,584 bytes** under `$HOME/.claude/projects/-tmp-vs-mmjump-test-<pid>`, the fixed one **0**.

**A THIRD SUITE HAD THE ANSWER ALREADY.** `test-desktop-resume-paging.mjs` — same class, same synthetic transcript, bigger — passes `HOME: fakeHome` to its server and writes its fixture there. It is the template the other two were fixed to. And `test-chat-e2e.mjs` (one REAL haiku turn per push, so the CLI writes a REAL transcript under the developer's home) removed its project dir at the very END of the happy path, after four `process.exit(1)` returns and with no signal handler — 155 of the 222 dead cursors are its throwaway cwds. Measured on a throwaway HOME: even on the happy path it left 324 bytes of CLI state the cleanup never named.

**THE FIX IS TWO-SIDED, AND ONLY THE SECOND HALF SURVIVES A LEFTOVER.**
① **ISOLATION.** `scripts/scratch.mjs` gains `scratchHome(name, fs)` and `fixtureSid(suffix)`; every suite that spawns a server and writes a transcript passes `HOME: fakeHome`, removes that home in `cleanup`, and installs SIGTERM/SIGINT/SIGHUP handlers beside `process.on('exit')`. `test-chat-e2e` is isolated too — **measured, not assumed**: claude 2.1.263 in the chat-wrapper's exact flag shape serves a full turn under a throwaway `$HOME` with the oat riding `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` alone (`system/init` → `assistant` → `result/success`, 2.4 s, no onboarding gate, transcript in that home), and the account this suite seeds is oat-only by construction, so `resolveForSpawn` takes the `oatOnly` branch and `HOME` is not in `AGENT_ENV_DROP`. Its cleanup is now unconditional and it sweeps its own stale leftovers at start, exactly like the wire probe's r5/r6 contract.
② **NO PRODUCTION READER COUNTS A FIXTURE.** `src/fixture-guard.js` (PURE) is the ONE declaration of the convention — the `vs-` scratch prefix under a tmp root, and the `e2e00000-0000-4000-8000-` session-id family. `src/usage-walker.js` and `src/session-store.js` ask it; the shipped single-file scanner `data/bin/vibespace-usage-scan` carries an INLINE COPY (a checkout-less ssh host cannot require `src/`), driven over one table by `scripts/test-usage-walk-parity.mjs`. ① alone is not enough: a suite killed by a signal, an OOM, or a `git worktree` cleanup that never reached its handler leaves the fixture behind, and the production instance picks it up seconds later.
③ **THE HISTORY.** Migration `2026-09-purge-test-fixture-ledger` (`src/fixture-ledger-purge.js`) archives every fixture row to `data/archive/fixture-ledger-rows-<date>.ndjson` with a reason, drops the dead cursors and the synthetic attribution entries, VOIDS the `costSince` of every anchor whose interval contained a removed row (the reading is real; the cost measured beside it is not — the same move `src/reading-repair.js` makes), and drops `rates.json` so the estimator re-learns. **Measured on a COPY of this instance's stores (2026-09-09 14:39 UTC):** 573,802 rows in → **79,778 removed** (79,533 fabricated / 245 real-turn-in-a-fixture-cwd) → 494,024 out, 79,780 archive lines (conservation checked by line count), 222 cursors, 0 attribution, **1,575 anchors voided across 7 files**, rates dropped, 0 fixture rows left anywhere, idempotent on a second run, 1.26 s.
④ **THE STANDING SWEEP.** `scripts/test-fixture-isolation.mjs`, LAST in the fast tier, runs the pure rule over the real `~/.claude/projects` and PRINTS it. Its censuses are grep-derived over all 197 suites and printed: (a) no suite WRITES under the real `~/.claude` (reading it is fine — this suite and the corpus measurement in test-codex-effort-meta do exactly that), (b) every suite that spawns `server.js` AND writes a transcript NAMES a `HOME` in that spawn env, (c) the suites carrying a synthetic sid mint it, handle signals and census the real home themselves. Its strongest control is the RETIRED BYTES: `git show master:scripts/test-chat-paging.mjs` through the shared sanitized git env, asserting the census catches both offenders exactly as they shipped — and a tree git cannot read is a loud SKIP, never a red.

**THE COST, STATED.** Skipping the whole convention also stops ledgering the handful of REAL turns the CI probes run in a throwaway cwd: 245 rows of genuine machine-login spend over the ledger's lifetime, 0.04 % of its 573,802 rows, against 79,533 fabricated ones. Those transcripts are deleted by their own suites seconds later, so the ledger was keeping rows for conversations the product itself removes. Token counts are reported PER CLASS (`syntheticTokens` 982,140 vs `fixtureCwdTokens` 6,147,245 — the latter dominated by cache reads) precisely so the fabricated half cannot hide inside the real one.

**WHAT COULD NOT BE ISOLATED.** `scripts/probe-claude-stdout.mjs` measures what the INSTALLED CLI puts on our stdout with the machine's real credentials, so a throwaway `$HOME` would measure a different install. It stays the ONE entry in `REAL_HOME_FIXTURE_PREFIXES`, with its reason, and the sweep spares its dirs only while they are younger than `FIXTURE_STALE_MS` — a constant the probe now READS from the guard instead of declaring its own copy, because two hand-written copies of one threshold is how the sweep and the probe would come to disagree about the same directory (the probe's own r6/r7 lesson, one layer up). Older one-off fixture names outside the `vs-` convention (`-tmp-vsv-probe`, `-tmp-otel-cap-cwd-*`, a handful of hand-run experiments) are deliberately OUT of scope: the guard is narrow on purpose and the sweep says so rather than widening silently.

**INVARIANTS.** **A test fixture that a production reader can see is production data.** The isolation and the refusal are two halves of one fix, and only the refusal survives the case where no cleanup runs — so build both, and never accept "it cleans up afterwards" as the whole answer for a window in which another process is reading. **A suite that spawns a server must NAME the home that server gets**; inheriting one is a decision nobody wrote down. **`process.on('exit')` is half a cleanup** — the signals that end a test run in anger do not fire it. **A synthetic record that carries a `usage` block is a claim about money**, so the id family it uses belongs in a shared constant that the production walk refuses by name — the only evidence that survives a fixture with no cwd. **Decide with a MEASUREMENT whether isolation is possible before assuming it is not** (the real CLI serving a turn under a throwaway home on the oat alone is a two-minute experiment that turned "it must keep the real home" into "it never needed it"). **Report a repair's token counts per CLASS** when the classes mean different things; one number lets fabrication hide inside real spend. And **an exemption must be paid for**: the sweep exempts itself from two of its own censuses because its CONTROLS spell the forbidden shapes inline — so it asserts instead that its real-home section contains no write call and that every child it starts is `git`, and that each exemption is still LIVE (a dead one fails, and one of these proofs was already falsified once, within the same afternoon, by adding the control that spawns `git`).

**GATES.** `scripts/test-fixture-isolation.mjs` (54: the pure rule, the sweep decision, a planted fixture under a throwaway home with the walk refusing it and a real project dir still walking as its negative control, the three grep censuses with pre-fix synthetic controls and the retired-bytes control) · `scripts/test-usage-walk-parity.mjs` (40: both spellings refuse the same fixture tree and agree over an 11-name × 6-id table, non-vacuously) · `scripts/test-migrations.mjs` (29: the purge over a disk-shaped fixture — an unparseable line is never removed, the two classes carry different reasons, only the anchors whose interval contained a removed row are voided, a clean instance is untouched and not notified) · plus the per-suite census inside test-chat-paging, test-minimap-jump, test-desktop-resume-paging and test-chat-e2e.

**r2 — THE CENSUS THAT CERTIFIED A CLEAN SHEET WHILE AN `rm -rf` OF THE REAL HOME STOOD ONE ALIAS AWAY** (round-1 verifier, reproduced first).

**THE DEFECT.** Census (a) — the assert whose whole job is *no suite writes under the REAL `~/.claude`* — matched a write only when its FIRST ARGUMENT was literally the bound name or `path.join(<bound name>, …)`. `scripts/test-chat-e2e.mjs` binds `REAL_PROJECTS = path.join(os.homedir(), '.claude', 'projects')`, then does `const p = path.join(REAL_PROJECTS, d.name)` and `fs.rmSync(p, { recursive: true, force: true })` — a recursive DELETE under the developer's own home, ONE hop of aliasing away, and the census could not see it. Reproduced by extracting the shipped `realHomeWriters` VERBATIM out of the suite's own bytes and running it over that file: `bound names ["REAL_PROJECTS"]`, `census hits []`; the live suite printed `census (a): walked 195 suites (+2 exempt …)` followed by `✓ no suite writes under the REAL ~/.claude`. The round-1 commit and the sweep's own header both sell that census as the enforcement of the isolation rule, and the report disclosed only its HOME-spelling limit, never its write-TARGET-spelling limit. **HONEST BOUNDARY:** today's blast radius is bounded by the suite's own filter (`isFixtureProjectDir` ∧ the `chat-e2e` substring ∧ `ageMs > FIXTURE_STALE_MS`), so no real conversation was ever at risk — the defect is that the write is UNDECLARED, UNPINNED and STRUCTURALLY INVISIBLE, so widening that filter, or any future indirect write in any of the 197 suites, lands with no red.

**THIS CENSUS HAS NOW BEEN WIDENED TWICE, AND BOTH MISSES WERE "ONE MORE HOP."** Round 1 widened the HOME spelling (it knew only `os.homedir()`; the one suite that genuinely writes there spells it `process.env.HOME || os.homedir()`). This round is the TARGET spelling. So the resolver no longer stops at a hop count: it takes the FIXPOINT of "a name derived from a name already known to be rooted at the real home" (the known name as the HEAD of a right-hand side — plain alias, `X + '/y'`, `` `${X}/y` `` — or anywhere inside a `path.join`/`path.resolve`), and the write matcher asks whether the call's FIRST ARGUMENT mentions such a name at all (`[^,)]` cannot cross into a later argument, so `fs.writeFileSync(elsewhere, X)` is still not a write TO X). Deliberately over-inclusive, and the cost was MEASURED rather than assumed: over all 197 suites the transitive rule flags exactly one file the narrow one missed (test-chat-e2e) and no others.

**THE EXEMPTION IS SCOPED TO A BLOCK, NOT TO THE FILE.** test-chat-e2e's sweep is legitimate — it removes ITS OWN pre-fix leftovers from the real home, exactly like the wire probe — so it joins `WRITE_EXEMPT`. But an exemption keyed to a FILE turns the census off for the whole file, and the exemption is the dangerous half of a census. Its write is one block, so it pays with the tighter property the shape allows: sentinels (`// >>> real-home sweep` … `// <<< real-home sweep`) mark it, and the payment RE-RUNS THE CENSUS'S OWN RULE over the file with that region CUT OUT, requiring the remainder to be EMPTY — a second real-home write anywhere else in that suite goes red even though the suite is on the list — plus a pin that the block only DELETES and only behind all three gates (the declared convention, the suite's own name, the shared staleness threshold). test-stdout-registry keeps its FILE-scoped exemption because its writes are spread and the property that bounds ITS damage is a different one (the declared `vs-wire-probe-` prefix + a cleanup that survives a signal).

**r2 INVARIANTS.** **A text census resolves exactly the spellings its author happened to look at** — when the same class of miss recurs, stop adding the next spelling and make the resolver TRANSITIVE, then measure the widening's cost across the whole corpus instead of arguing it. **A grep-derived guard has TWO spelling surfaces, not one** (where the dangerous root comes from, and how the dangerous value reaches the call), and fixing only the one the incident happened to expose leaves the other as the next incident. **An exemption is the dangerous half of a census** — scope it to the smallest unit the code shape allows (a sentinel-marked block, re-checked with the census's own rule), because a file-scoped exemption silently blesses every future write in that file. **A guard that certifies a clean sheet must state what it CANNOT see**, or its green line is a stronger claim than the code behind it.

**r2 GATES.** `scripts/test-fixture-isolation.mjs` 69 — census (a) now walks 194 suites (+3 exempt), the exemption is asserted LIVE (which IS the regression assert: revert the resolver and that line goes red on the real file) and PAID FOR in two halves, and the widening carries seven controls: the one-hop shape byte-for-byte from test-chat-e2e, a two-hop shape, a template-literal derivation both bound and inline, an aliased READ that must STILL not be flagged, a real-home path used as CONTENT in a later argument, and the region rule's three cases (inside is covered, a second write outside is not, a missing sentinel FAILS rather than passing vacuously). Five mutations, each red in exactly the assert whose job it is: the shipped narrow resolver (red on the live exemption AND four controls), the exemption removed (red on the census itself), a second real-home write outside the sentinels (payment ①), the sweep's own-name gate dropped (payment ②), and the sentinels removed (both payments).

**r3 — THE SWEEP DEMANDED A REMOVAL NOTHING IN THIS TREE IS ALLOWED TO PERFORM** (r2 verifier, reproduced first).

**THE DEFECT.** `fixtureLitter` gave an in-flight tolerance only to a DECLARED prefix (`if (why && ageMs <= staleMs)`); every other fixture name fell straight through to `offenders.push(...)` regardless of age. So a fixture project dir a CONCURRENT checkout put in the real home turned the MANDATORY pre-push gate red in every OTHER worktree on the box. REPRODUCED live at e1dd1e83 while running the full fast tier: `LITTER: [{"name":"-tmp-vs-chat-e2e-cwd-paw5IT","ageMs":261348,"declared":false}]` → `✗ test-fixture-isolation` → exit 1. ATTRIBUTION, measured not assumed: the mkdtemp suffix (`paw5IT`) is a shape only `master:scripts/test-chat-e2e.mjs`'s `fs.mkdtempSync(...)` can mint — the fixed tree mints `/tmp/vs-chat-e2e-cwd-<pid>` through `scratch()` and contains no `mkdtempSync` at all. This box hosts ~160 checkouts of this repo and the heavy tier is DETACHED, so overlapping runs are the normal case, not the edge one.

**WHY IT IS A DEFECT AND NOT THE FEATURE — three independent reasons.** (a) The same branch writes the OPPOSITE rule thirty lines away: test-chat-e2e's own sweep spares a leftover younger than the threshold because *"it may be a CONCURRENT copy of this suite — two worktrees pushing minutes apart really do overlap."* Every sweeper in this tree has that floor, so at the moment the gate went red, **nothing in the repo was permitted to remove the directory it was demanding be removed** — the human was told to run `rm -rf` for a dir the automation would clear on its own. (b) The repo already records this exact shape as an incident (test-stdout-registry round 5: an ABSOLUTE-ABSENCE assertion turned the mandatory gate red on the very case the sweep exists to spare), and its fix was to make the readers honour the sweeper's own rule. (c) At the moment it goes red the offender is provably INERT under this branch: `runUsageWalk` ingests 0 events from it and discovery skips it by the same predicate — the red was blocking a push while protecting nothing. Self-heal exists but is PARTIAL: only test-chat-e2e sweeps, and only names containing `chat-e2e`; a stale leftover from the other three families has no sweeper anywhere.

**THE FIX IS THE FLOOR THE TREE ALREADY HAD, MADE OPT-IN.** `fixtureLitter(entries, { graceMs })` — an UNDECLARED fixture dir younger than `graceMs` is SPARED and NAMED (`declared:false` + `IN_FLIGHT_WHY`, which says why nothing may remove it yet), and becomes an offender the moment it goes stale. The grace is passed by EXACTLY ONE caller — §4, the whole-directory standing sweep, at the shared `FIXTURE_STALE_MS` — and by nothing else. The four PER-SUITE censuses keep absolute strictness on purpose: they hand this function entries already diffed against a listing taken before their own run, whose mtimes are by construction ≈ now, so a grace there would spare the suite's OWN litter — the vacuous direction. That is also why the branch is guarded on `graceMs > 0`: with the default 0, an entry whose mtime equals `now` has `ageMs === 0` and `0 <= 0` would have quietly relaxed every strict caller. Deliberately NOT done: teaching the census to delete the other three families' stale residue — that turns a read-only measurement into a writer and would break the §4 read-only property census (a) is paid with; the printed `rm -rf` remedy stays for genuinely stale litter.

**r3 INVARIANTS.** **A gate may not demand a removal that every sweeper in the tree is forbidden to perform** — when a check and a cleaner disagree about the same directory, the check is the one that is wrong, and the threshold it must adopt is the cleaner's, not a new one. **An absolute-absence assertion over a SHARED resource is a claim about every other process on the box**; second strike of that shape here, so the tolerance is derived from the existing constant rather than invented. **A relaxation is scoped by its blast radius, not by convenience** — it belongs to the one caller that looks at a resource it did not diff, and a WIRING PIN (grep-derived, printed) asserts BOTH halves: no other suite passes it, and the sweep still does. **`0` is a real value in a threshold comparison** — an opt-in bound must test for opt-in, or the default silently relaxes every strict caller. **A spare is not a shrug**: the entry is printed with its age and a reason naming what is and is not known about it.

**r3 GATES.** `scripts/test-fixture-isolation.mjs` 78 — §2b drives the reproduced 4-minute leftover through the real rule (spared, `declared:false`, reason names the concurrency), with three negative controls (the SAME dir past the threshold is still an offender; the per-suite call shape with NO grace flags a brand-new dir including `ageMs === 0`; a DECLARED prefix keeps its own reason and is never absorbed into the grace) plus a pin that the decision reports the grace it applied; §6 is the wiring pin with both controls. END-TO-END on the reproduction itself (the real suite bytes, `os.homedir()` pointed at a throwaway home carrying the planted leftover): 4 min old ⇒ exit 0 and the entry NAMED; the same dir back-dated 15 min ⇒ exit 1 with the `rm -rf` remedy. Four mutations, each red in exactly the assert whose job it is: the grace branch removed (the r2 shape), `graceMs > 0` dropped (the `0 <= 0` trap), §4's option removed (the relaxation unwired), and a per-suite census relaxing itself.


## THE QUEUE STRIP SHOWED A MESSAGE THAT HAD RUN AN HOUR EARLIER, AND CLICKING ✕ PAINTED IT RED (2026-09-09, owner report on a live codex session)

**WHAT THE ARTIFACTS SAY.** One codex session (`queueVerbs` full, wrapper caps complete). Its buffer: `04:28:23.432Z queue_changed [A]` → `04:28:35.394Z queue_op_result steer ok` → `04:28:35.395Z queue_changed []` → **58 minutes with no queue record at all** → `05:20` the server restarts → `05:25:51.216Z queue_op_result op:remove id:A ok:false reason:'gone'`. That last line is the owner clicking ✕ on a row for a message that had left the queue nearly an hour and one restart earlier; the answer was the toast *"That message is no longer queued — it already ran."* and the row went **red** (`data-queue-state="refused"`) and stayed there. Two separate things are wrong: a client was holding a row nobody could act on, and the one verdict that could have retired it MARKED it instead.

**THE SERVER COULD NOT HAVE HELPED — IT WAS GUESSING.** A queue publication is a `queue_changed` record on the wrapper's stdout, and stdout is a RING in every wrapper that owns a queue (`MAX_BUFFER = 800000`, head-dropped). A restarted server rebuilds the normalizer from exactly that buffer merged with the harness transcript — and the harness transcript never carries our queue records at all. Measured on the report's own session: a read-only replay of the restart rebuild (`CodexSessionMessages(...).raw()` → `createMessageManager('codex').convertHistoryAsync`) holds **6894 records and ZERO** `queue_changed` / `queue_op_result` / `queued_input`. So `queueState()` was `[]` and `queuePublished()` was false — and the attach payload's `queue: []` was **byte-identical to the answer for a wrapper whose queue really is empty**. The failure mode was not staleness, it was *ignorance presented as a fact*, and it is wrong in BOTH directions: this session's ghost row, and its mirror image where a wrapper really is holding a queued message and the strip shows nothing.

**AND THE CLIENT KEPT ITS OWN COPY ACROSS A RECONNECT.** `_reattach`'s SAME-EPOCH branch — the one attach path that does not rebuild the view — was written for `chatStatus` and stopped there. It dropped every other live fact the payload carries: the input queue, `queueSupported`/`queueVerbs`, turnState, inProgressTools, autoResume, outputStyle, responseStyleLive, spawnOrigin. It is reachable twice over — the delivery-stall watchdog calls `_reattach()` with the epoch already current, and an ordinary reconnect with no server restart is same-epoch by definition — and reproduced from the real module: a re-attach carrying `queue: []` leaves the ghost row on the strip and never even tells the composer. **HONEST BOUNDARY:** which of the client's reconnects that particular window took at 05:20 is not recoverable from the artifacts; what IS reproduced is that this branch keeps a strip the server no longer knows about, that a `gone` verdict paints the row red instead of removing it, and that after a restart the server cannot answer the question at all.

**THE FIX — the process that owns the fact answers for it.** ① The payload says WHICH it is: `queueKnown` (`src/ws-handler.js`'s `queueAdvert` block; `created` sends false too — the app-server's queue belongs to the THREAD, so a RESUMED thread hands a new wrapper items it never filled). ② A new stdin verb `queue-resync` — "state your queue again, out loud" — served by both wrappers that own a queue, formatted by the ADAPTER (`formatQueueResync`; the base class refuses), making **no RPC** (the wrapper re-states what it already believes, so an attach never waits on a wedged app-server) and using **`force`**, because `publishQueue` dedups on the wrapper's own fingerprint and "still empty" is precisely the sentence it cannot otherwise say. A resync that lands while the Stop sweep owns the publish is OWED and paid after it. ③ The attach ASKS, only while it does not know, behind the harness caps row AND the running wrapper's own `queueResync` advert — the second gate matters more than usual here because the ACP wrapper answers an unknown verb with a VISIBLE error card, so asking an older process would put a red notice in the chat on every attach. ④ The client: `_setQueue(items, {known})` applies the rows either way (an unknown queue IS the empty list on the wire, and one round trip of no rows beats a row nobody can act on) but suppresses the INFERENCE — on a KNOWN list it retires the `Queued` chip of every rendered bubble the queue does not list, on an unknown one it retires nothing, because we did not learn that the message left, we learned that we do not know. ⑤ A `'gone'` verdict REMOVES the row (and the local twin retires that bubble's chip): absence is a fact from the one process that owns the queue, and a `refused` marker there is permanent — the wrapper's own follow-up `refreshQueue()` cannot correct it, since by its fingerprint nothing changed. Every other refusal keeps the row: that message really is still queued. ⑥ The same-epoch re-attach applies `_applyLiveMeta(msg)`, which is carries-the-key guarded throughout, so it can only replace a fact the server just stated.

**INVARIANTS.** A value computed from records the computer may never have seen must state whether it is a FACT or a GUESS — "how fresh" is the wrong question when the failure mode is "did we ever know", and the two answers are the same bytes. The process that OWNS a fact must be able to re-state it ON DEMAND, including the EMPTY answer, and the ask is gated on that process's own advert, never on a backend id. An answer that must be repeatable cannot ride a deduping publisher (`force`, or the re-statement is swallowed exactly when it is needed); a latch that owns a publisher OWES the asks it swallowed. Absence reported by the owner of a thing is a FACT — remove the row, do not mark it; a marker for a row that should not exist is state that outlives its subject. A guard that suppresses an INFERENCE must not also suppress the DATA (the rows still apply; only the chip reconciliation is held). And a branch written for one field of a snapshot silently drops the other eight — an attach path either applies the payload's live half through the ONE shared applier or it is a whitelist waiting to drift (sixth strike in this family).

**GATES.** `scripts/test-queue-steer.mjs` ⑬ (mechanism + client, with pre-fix controls patched out of the real modules) and ⑭ (END TO END: worktree server, the REAL wrapper under dtach against a stub app-server, the ring rolled by a real long reply and measured on the buffer file, SIGKILL + reboot, a negative-control boot with the ask gated off against the SAME wrappers that corrects nothing, then the fixed boot correcting both directions, and a headless browser where the ghost strip ends empty, a real pending row comes back, and a real ✕ click's `gone` removes its row without ever painting it red — the same wire frame then replayed into a pre-fix client copy, which paints it). `scripts/test-harness-contract.mjs` holds the conformance row (ask + answer + advert, per harness) and `scripts/test-codex-p2-wrapper.mjs` ⑩ the wrapper verb against a real wrapper (including the control that a `thread/queue/changed` over an unchanged queue publishes NOTHING — the reason waiting can never fix this).

**r2 — THE MIRROR HALF: THE PAYLOAD ARRIVED BEFORE THE ANSWER IT ASKED FOR, AND WAS APPLIED AFTER IT** (round-1 verifier, reproduced first). The round-1 fix was measured on windows OPENED AFTER the reboot, where the payload is applied the instant it lands and therefore always wins. The reported shape is the other one — a window that STAYED OPEN across the restart — and it never reaches the same-epoch branch at all: a restart always changes `normEpoch` (`session._normEpoch = Date.now()`, boot-restore), so `_reattach` takes the epoch-CHANGED branch, which DEFERS the whole payload behind `Math.random() * 500` ms (2.338.0's render stagger, so N windows do not re-render their tails in one tick). The resync that this same attach asked for is answered by the wrapper in ~10 ms. So the guess landed LAST and wiped the answer it provoked: measured 12/12 in a focused harness at a 10 ms answer, and 7/10 in the verifier's own end-to-end run (it is an ordering race, right whenever the timer happened to fire first). **The damage is PERMANENT for that window, which is what makes it the ghost's mirror rather than a blink: the ask is SELF-LIMITING, so the server — which by then holds the row and hands it to the NEXT attach as a fact — never asks again.** A message that will really run then sits on the wire, in the server, and nowhere on screen: no row to steer, remove or edit.

**THE FIX IS RECENCY, and deliberately not "known beats unknown".** `_setQueue` gains `at` — WHEN THIS STATEMENT ARRIVED — and refuses any statement older than the newest one already applied (`_queueStatedAt`); `_reattach` stamps the frame with `msg.__rxTick` (`performance.now()`, monotonic, because the only question ever asked of these two numbers is which arrived first) BEFORE the branch hands it to a timer, and `_applyLiveMeta` reads it. Absent ⇒ now, which is the truth for every caller that applies a frame the moment it lands. A "known wins" rule would have been wrong: the rows the placeholder has to clear are themselves a KNOWN list — the pre-restart client's — and clearing them is the whole of round 1. Recency is the ONLY property that separates the two cases, and both directions are measured. The same comparison closes a second instance nobody reported: a steer that empties the queue INSIDE the stagger window used to be undone by the deferred payload's older KNOWN rows.

**r2 INVARIANTS.** A DEFERRED application is judged by when its INPUT ARRIVED, never by when it ran — a snapshot handed to a timer must carry its own instant, or "last executed wins" quietly makes the newest fact the loser (and every jitter/stagger/debounce in the codebase is a place where that sentence is false by default). That instant rides ON THE FRAME (the payload IS the meta) — a second out-of-band channel beside it is the whitelist-drift class. When a fix's two directions are "clear THIS list" and "keep THAT one", the flag that NAMES the two cases is usually not the property that separates them; find the one that does. A SELF-LIMITING ask turns a transient race into a PERMANENT loss — once the answer flips the flag nothing asks again, so a race inside the window that provoked the answer is not a blink and must not be reasoned about as one. And two test-methodology invariants, both of which cost a wrong-green first: a browser leg that opens FRESH windows after the restart is structurally incapable of seeing this, so the surviving-window case must keep a window OPEN across the SIGKILL; and an end-to-end leg's PREMISE must be re-established rather than inherited — the first cut of the ⑭b arms ran after several resyncs had written fresh `queue_changed` records back into that same ring, so the reboot rebuilt a normalizer that DID know, the placeholder never happened, and both arms "passed" for the wrong reason.

**r2 GATES.** `scripts/test-queue-steer.mjs` ⑬d (deterministic, no browser: the REAL `_reattach` with the stagger PINNED — an ordering race must be pinned, not sampled, or the control is a coin toss — both orderings, a pre-fix module with ONLY the guard line removed, the ghost direction still cleared in BOTH copies so the measurement is of the guard and not of a placeholder this round disabled, the rule's two directions on the ONE writer, and the known-vs-known twin) and ⑭b (END TO END on the SAME live sessions and real dtach wrappers as ⑭: exactly one window per session — a second view would answer the self-limiting ask first — the ring re-rolled by a real long reply and measured on the buffer file, the stagger pinned in the page, `_setQueue` instrumented as a pass-through recorder, and the two arms built from the same file differing by ONE line, each into its own bundle, with WHICH CODE THE PAGE IS RUNNING read off the loaded prototype rather than hoped about. Both arms record the SAME two calls in the SAME order — the wrapper's answer, then the stale placeholder — and only the outcome differs: PRE-FIX the strip ends EMPTY, FIXED the row is still there). 828 asserts. `scripts/test-auto-resume.mjs`'s carries-the-key sweep reads every `meta.<key>` in `_applyLiveMeta`, which is why `__rxTick` is spelled with its own `in meta` test even though its absent case resets nothing — and the drift pin that had bounded two lines by a BYTE BUDGET (red twice now, once per added comment) is restated as ORDER + SHAPE.

**r3 — THE SAME RACE ON THE LINE ABOVE, AND THE PRE-FIX COPY THAT DIRTIES THE TREE** (round-2 verifier, both reproduced first).

**① THE ADVERT WAS NEVER GIVEN THE RULE.** r2 handed recency to the queue ROWS and left the line one above them — the ADVERT (`queueSupported` / `queueVerbs`) — judged by "whatever ran last". That is not a lesser fact: `_queueCaps()` collapses to `NO_QUEUE_CAPS` when the flag is false, and `ChatInput._renderQueue` then does `const items = this._queueCaps.queueOps ? this._queue : []` and HIDES the strip. So a stale `queueSupported:false` reproduces r2's own outcome exactly — a real pending message the wrapper holds, present in `_queue`, rendered NOWHERE — with r2's guard intact and green beside it. Round 1 filed this as an admitted open issue with "no reproduction", on the reasoning that "the advert comes from the running wrapper's sidecar and does not change under a restart, and `_setQueueSupported` early-returns when nothing changed". That reasoning is true only for a LOCAL session with a READABLE sidecar. ws-handler names the other case where it asks (`or REMOTE — its sidecar lives on ITS machine`), and the 2.339.2 sidecar-resolution-failure class is a third; there the advert falls back to the IN-BAND publication, which a restart RESETS. Reproduced to this branch's own evidentiary standard — the REAL `wrapperCaps` answering `{inputQueue:false, queueResync:false, reason:'no-sidecar'}`, the REAL `queueAdvert` block LIFTED out of ws-handler and RUN over a REBUILT normalizer (returning `{queueSupported:false, queueVerbs:null, queueKnown:true}`), and that exact payload driven through the REAL `ChatView.prototype._reattach` on the epoch-CHANGED branch with the stagger PINNED at ~495 ms against the wrapper's own publication at 10 ms: `{ids:['qReal'], supported:false, queueOps:false, stripCalls:[{n:1,ops:true},{n:1,ops:false}]}`. The reversed order is correct in both copies, so it is an ORDERING race of exactly the family r2 closed.

**THE FIX IS THE SAME RULE FROM THE SAME STAMP, WITH ITS OWN CLOCK.** `rxAt` is computed ONCE in `_applyLiveMeta`, hoisted ABOVE the advert, and handed to both writers. `_setQueueSupported(next, verbs, { at })` keeps its OWN `_queueAdvertStatedAt` — deliberately not `_queueStatedAt`, because `_dropQueueRow` stamps the ROWS at `now` from a purely local inference, and one shared clock would let a statement about the rows censor a statement about the advert for no reason at all. **And the guard sits ABOVE the no-change early return**, which is load-bearing rather than tidy: the wrapper's answer is usually a no-CHANGE (same process, so the pre-restart view already holds `supported:true` with the same verbs), so a guard below that return never records the answer's instant and the stale payload still wins — measured `{supported:false, queueOps:false}` below vs `{supported:true, queueOps:true}` above.

**② THE BRANCH'S OWN PRE-FIX COPY COULD BLOCK THE RELEASE GATE.** ⑬d writes `src/lib/.chat-view.stale-prefix-<pid>.js` into `src/lib/` (it must be a SIBLING or its relative imports do not resolve) with NEITHER of the two protections this repo has already written down for exactly this idiom — no gitignore entry and no start-of-run PID sweep, only a `finally` unlink, which a SIGKILL does not run. Measured: `git status --porcelain` prints `?? src/lib/.chat-view.stale-prefix-99999.js`, `node scripts/ci.mjs --heavy` answers `REFUSED — this tree is DIRTY, so a marker for <sha> would not describe what ran` (exit 2) and the fast tier likewise writes no green marker; the stranded copy also sits inside the directory test-turn-truth-ui's leg ⓪ walks. Both precedents (`src/server/vs-wake-mut-*.js`, `src/server/vs-aredge-mut-*.js`) carry the explanation verbatim in .gitignore, so this one is written the same way, and ⑬d sweeps by PID LIVENESS at the top — only PIDs that are GONE, because this suite can legitimately run twice in one worktree and deleting a live run's module mid-import is worse than the litter. The sweep reports its own verdict (`removed` / `spared`) and the assert reads THAT rather than inventing a second rule.

**r3 INVARIANTS.** A fix's scope is the OUTCOME it repairs, not the field it first looked at — every fact that decides whether the same thing is rendered owes the same rule ("does this strip have controls" and "which rows does this strip have" are two halves of one user-visible outcome). Two different facts need two stamps: one clock lets either censor the other, and the writer that makes a purely local inference will do it routinely. The position of an early return is load-bearing — a recency guard placed below "return if nothing changed" fails precisely when the answer is a no-change, which is the common case, so the position itself must be MEASURED and independently mutation-red. An open issue filed as "no reproduction" must name the premise its reasoning rests on; this one was true for local sessions and false for remote, which is why it was filed instead of fixed. The two protections for writing patched copies into the source tree are a PAIR (gitignore for a SIGKILL's litter, the PID sweep against accumulation) and an assert that cannot fail is not an assert — both halves are mutation-red here. And one methodology note: a control that could not be BUILT deserves one loud red, never an `import(undefined)` — while mutation-testing this very arm, a missing needle took every remaining assert in the file with it (4 clean reds after the fix).

**r3 GATES.** `scripts/test-queue-steer.mjs` 842 — ⑬d ⑥ (the REAL `wrapperCaps` + the REAL `queueAdvert` + the REAL `_reattach`, a pre-fix copy with ONLY the advert guard removed, the reversed-order negative control, the rule's two directions, and the separate-stamps assert) plus the sweep's positive/negative controls and the gitignore assert; the two drift pins that named the old call text and the old signature are restated. Mutation-tested against the product source: removing the advert guard ⇒ 4 red (suite still completes); moving it BELOW the early return ⇒ 2 red; removing the .gitignore stanza ⇒ 1 red. Also green: test-auto-resume 221 (its carries-the-key sweep reads the rewritten `_applyLiveMeta` body), test-harness-contract 316, test-codex-p2-wrapper 401, test-attach-rebuild 55, test-acp-harness 135, test-chat-trim-guard 112, test-worktree-userchan-ui 85.
## A RESTORED SESSION WENT DARK UNTIL SOMEBODY TYPED (2026-09-09, production journal)

**THE SHAPE.** systemd stopped vibespace at 05:20:14Z for an in-app update to 2.369.77. The new server said `Found 12 existing session(s), reconnecting...` at 05:20:22Z and `Ready.` at 05:20:53Z (with a `[ws] heartbeat round tainted (tick 4s late, longest loop gap 30s)` across that boot). At 05:20:56Z it spawned the local daemon; the singleton that answered was the OLD one, so `[agentd] daemon 2.369.75 != 2.369.77 - upgrading (attempt 1/3)` streamed a new bundle into it, the daemon re-exec'd, and `device-daemon: device session routing ENABLED` landed at 05:21:02Z. The owner's claude session then showed `3 Bash - running...` for about **eight minutes** while its wrapper kept appending records to `data/session-buffers/<id>.buf` the whole time (05:20:17 ... 05:23:44 ...). The moment the owner typed, ws-handler's detector fired `Broken pty stdin detected - re-attaching dtach` (05:29:05Z; a second claude session the same way at 05:26:21Z on ITS first input) and the local re-attach flushed everything at once. A codex session that received a queue-op at 05:25:51Z did NOT log the detector - its wrapper acked - so at least some daemon channels worked and at least two did not.

**THE MECHANISM.** server.js constructs `deviceMgr` and calls `restoreSessions()` on the very next lines ("bring it up BEFORE restore so re-adopted sessions attach through it too"), so every restored session's `attachToDtach` took the daemon path *while the link was still being established* - and that path FIRE-AND-FORGOT the open. `deviceMgr.openSession()` resolves as soon as the `open-session` control frame is WRITTEN; the daemon's answer lives on `handle.ready` (`session-open` / `session-error` / the 2.271.0 T1-6 pre-ready link death), which nothing awaited, and `setupSessionPty` ran on the shim regardless. An open the daemon never completed is then a **silently dead pty**: no journal line, the session still LIVE in the sidebar, and nothing to re-attach because the shim's `onExit` never fires either. The one thing that could heal it was a detector that only runs on user INPUT - which is the exact opposite of the restart-survival promise, whose whole content is that you do not have to touch anything.

**HONEST LIMIT.** The journal carries neither a `session-error` nor an `[agentd] connection lost` in that window, so WHICH of the two silent shapes (a reply that never came, or one nobody was listening for) hit those sessions is NOT established. Both are the same dead bridge, both are covered, and the suite drives three fault shapes (refuses / never answers / answers then relays nothing) rather than claiming the one we cannot prove.

**THE FIX.** (1) **Observe the open**: race `handle.ready` against a bounded budget (5 s, against a measured 2-11 ms) and fall back to the local pty on rejection OR timeout, naming the session and the reason - never silently. (2) **Boot ordering**: take the daemon path only when the link is ALREADY live. Awaiting it would put the whole ~40 s spawn + version handshake + bundle upload + re-exec in front of every session's restore and stall the boot outright if the daemon never came up; migrating a working local pty onto the daemon afterwards is a teardown + re-attach on a bridge that is already relaying. (3) **A liveness PROBE on every attach**: `dtach -a` emits its redraw preamble to the attaching client within milliseconds even when the program inside is silent, so a healthy attach always produces a byte and a dead bridge never does - a READ, no stdin write, no wrapper cooperation, works for chat and terminal. A probe LINE was rejected on measurement: chat-wrapper acks any stdin line and then forwards an unrecognised JSON line to claude's stdin / wraps a non-JSON line as a user message, i.e. **a billed turn**, and the sessions this exists for are by construction running the previous release's wrapper. (4) **One healer**: the probe and ws-handler's input detector both call `reattachLocalPty`; the detector's "did anything come back" half now reads the same liveness STAMP instead of `session.buffer.length` (a strict subset - the terminal branch swallows the preamble outright).

**INVARIANTS.**
- **A promise nobody awaits is a failure mode nobody can see.** `openSession()` answering "the frame is written" is not the daemon answering "the session is open"; a bridge whose open was never observed has no error, no exit and no journal line - it just goes quiet, which is indistinguishable from a quiet session.
- **A guard that only runs on user input cannot guard a promise about NOT needing the user.** Restart survival means output flows with zero input; the detector that healed this was structurally unable to fire until the promise had already been broken for eight minutes.
- **Probe by READING something the transport always produces, not by WRITING something the peer must interpret.** The write had a real price (a billed turn on a wrapper too old to know the verb); the read - dtach's own redraw kickoff - costs nothing, needs no cooperation, and is the same evidence for every mode.
- **A timing verdict taken during a known event-loop stall must be confirmed after the loop breathes.** Timers run before poll, so a 30 s boot gap fires "no bytes arrived" ahead of bytes that arrived long ago; the confirm stage is one extra timer, and it is the difference between a probe and a coin flip.
- **The tie in a millisecond comparison belongs to whichever side is cheaper to be wrong about.** Healing RE-SENDS the user's keystroke, so a byte stamped in the same millisecond as `since` reads ALIVE (`>=`): a missed heal gets another chance 4.6 s later, a duplicated keystroke does not get taken back. The predicate this replaced compared a monotone byte COUNT and had no tie to lose.
- **A duck that stands in for node-pty must hold a listener SET.** `daemonPtyShim` kept ONE `onData` slot, so registering the liveness stamp first would have silently REPLACED the whole stdout consumer - the fix's own instrumentation deleting the feature it instruments.
- **A channel's bytes can legally precede its "open" reply, and a promise settles a microtask too late to catch them.** The mux drains every frame in one read synchronously; buffer at the seam and replay when the listeners exist. Measured 0/8 same-read coalescing on this box, so the regression drives the shape directly through a fake handle and says so, instead of hoping for a race it cannot cause.
- **A control that cannot fail proves nothing.** The e2e leg's "the restored session streams NOTHING" is worthless unless the fixture is still printing, so an INDEPENDENT reader of the same dtach socket asserts that first - and the fixture command itself is a handshake with a retry, because typing `while :; do ...` into an interactive login shell at a fixed offset was measured landing mid-startup, parsed as a fragment, and answered `parse error near \`do'` (which is what made every zero below it vacuous on the first run).

**Gates.** scripts/test-restore-liveness.mjs (59: the probe's premise measured on both transports; the fault matrix - healthy / refused / never answered / answered-then-silent - each with a PRE-FIX control that is a patched copy of the real module; the early-byte seam with its own single-mechanism control; the boot-ordering gate with master's behaviour in the same window; an end-to-end restore over a real self-upgrading daemon with ZERO input; the one-healer wiring pins) + test-restore-smoke / test-local-device / test-agentd-session / test-session-schema / test-architecture.
## THE CEILING ON EVERY TURN NOBODY TYPED (2026-09-08 — docs/design-account-hardening.md §4.4c / P9, owner decisions D2 / D3 / D6 / D8)

This one has **no incident report**, which is the point: §1.4 of the design lists it under *"money that leaks with no incident attached"*, so it had to be MEASURED rather than argued.

**THE SHAPE.** Producers in this tree can start a BILLED turn with no per-occurrence owner action. Each carried a local floor — auto-resume's loop breaker (3 immediate fires per session per hour), the Stop nudge's `s._lastStopNudge`, the jobs engine's 30 s per-conversation flood floor. Not one of them is a bound on MONEY: they pace ONE producer, they are per SESSION while the bill belongs to the SUBSCRIPTION (nine conversations can be parked on one), and they live in memory on an instance that restarts several times a day.

**MEASURED**, on this instance's whole transcript corpus (8,087 files). A `find ~/.claude/projects -mtime -3` sample is a TRAP and this work fell into it first: `-mtime` selects **files**, and a long-lived conversation's file carries records from two months back, so the same grep reports "550 nudges in 3 days" when the truth is 550 over two months. Corrected:

```
603 Stop-nudge mini-turns · 72 conversations · 2026-07-10 → 2026-09-09
999 forced assistant records · 536,353,861 cache-read + 513,458 output tokens
peaks: 93 in one day · 184 in a rolling 72h · 21 on ONE conversation in ONE hour
```

(this instance runs `stopNudgeStaleMinutes=0` **and** `stopNudgeCooldownMinutes=0` — every-stop mode — which is exactly why the cooldown was never the bound). Alongside it, auto-resume's 130 billed continues in one night (its own essay below).

**THE FIX.** ONE authorizer: PURE `src/spend-authorizer.js` (the closed reason set, rolling hour/day windows, two-phase authorize/note, the 80 % notice, and `overageState` — the ONE reader of a record captured since 2.289.0 that had ZERO consumers) + ORCH `src/server/spend-guard.js` (persisted `data/spend-budget.json`, one journal line per (reason, identity, why) per 5 min, one "For you" item per (identity, why) per 6 h, telemetry). It is **constructed inside the pool engine**, because every input it needs is resolved there and its identity must be the engine's OWN `fireIdentityFor` — not a fifth derivation of session→account (§2 of the design: four rival derivations produced five incidents in three days). Wired at every producer: auto-resume (before the pre-fire gate AND again after it, on the identity the continue actually lands on — the gate can re-point the link), the delivery ladder for Background Work notifications and agent messages, the Stop nudge, and the codex reset credit.

Plus the three things §1.4 named and nobody read:

- **Paid overage** now refuses unattended spend (D3b). With overage on, `utilization` stays under 1 while every token is billed pay-per-use, so `accountRemaining()` ranked the account spending real money as the one with the MOST headroom. It is also rendered in BOTH quota panels (the taskbar popup and Manage Agents, where the owner picks a switch target), from the same PURE verdict. On this instance the record is present on 6 of 7 subscriptions and reads `inUse:false` (`org_level_disabled`) — dormant here by measurement, not by hope.
- **The EDF reserve floor** (D2, 15 % weekly by default): below it a member stops being a VOLUNTARY switch/placement target while a HARD-DEAD current member may still escape onto it, and the escape SAYS which bar it landed on. `poolBlockedNotice` learned the shape too — when a bar is the only thing that emptied the candidate list it says *"every other member is held back by your spending limits"* instead of "out of quota", which prescribes waiting for a reset that has nothing to do with it.
- **Both money gates fail CLOSED**: `beforeAutoResumeFire`'s `catch { return true; }` and the identical shape at the wiring site in server.js. Two sites, so fixing either one alone would have stayed green.

**INVARIANTS.** A floor on one producer's PACING is not a bound on MONEY · the unit of a spend ceiling is the CREDENTIAL SLOT the turn will bill, never the session · a ceiling that does not survive a restart is a scheduling detail (the Stop nudge's cooldown was an unregistered in-memory field the session-schema detector could not even SEE, because it matched only `session._x =` while the live session is passed around as `s`) · TWO PHASE, because an authorization that never became a turn must not consume budget · ignorance never blocks (an unreadable credential, an absent overage record) but an UNNAMEABLE IDENTITY does, since a ceiling nobody can be charged against is not a ceiling · a refusal must LOSE NOTHING (the delivery ladder stashes and the same words ride the conversation's next turn) and must always reach the user (journal + inbox, never a second in-chat card class) · every declared reason has a producer and every producer's reason is declared, both directions asserted, because a spare slot is what the next producer slides into without anyone deciding anything · and **measure the ceiling against the real history before claiming it fixes anything**.

**HONEST BOUNDARY (measured, not asserted).** Replayed through the real guard against those 603 nudges, the shipped D6 numbers refuse **20** (seven credential slots) to **55** (all on one slot) of them: this is a BACKSTOP against bursts and loops, not a routine throttle. The nudge exit condition saves nothing on that history either — 63 of the 72 nudged conversations received exactly one nudge each — it bounds the pathological long-lived non-reporting session, and its population is an upper bound because `data/session-status.json` keeps only the live sessions' keys.

**SIDE FIX, same class.** `scripts/test-session-schema.mjs` now reads ANY receiver (with a non-session allowlist whose rows fail when they stop matching), and the nine fields it could never see are registered with owners: `_lastStopNudge`, `_childPid`, `_restoreAgentTasks`, `_preambleSeen`, `_toolsIntroSeen`, `_mgrIntroSeen`, `_groupSeenAt`, `_ctxSig`, `_groupSnap`.

**r2 — FIVE REAL DEFECTS FOUND BY AN ADVERSARIAL VERIFIER (plus a sixth this branch found while verifying the fourth), EACH REPRODUCED BEFORE IT WAS FIXED (2026-09-09).** Full text in kb-file-structure under `spend-authorizer.js / server/spend-guard.js`. In one line each:
